 \zeta^2=C\lambda^2\ell^2\sum_d|d|d_*^2.$$ The latter quantity bounds its reference cost by <a href="#eq:profile-path-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-path-cost">[eq:profile-path-cost]</a>. For one completed macroscopic cluster define $\zeta_C^2$ by replacing $d_*$ with $M_C(d)$, and put $E_2=\sum_{\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}C\ge r}\zeta_C^2$. Weighted Jensen and <a href="#eq:profile-weighted-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-weighted-moments">[eq:profile-weighted-moments]</a> give $$\label{eq:profile-tilt-moments}
       \mathbb E_{W,N}\zeta^{2q}+\mathbb E_{W,N}E_2^q
        \le C_{q,\lambda,r}\ell^{2q}D_\ell^{2q}+o_k(1).$$ When $\zeta\le1$, the centered posterior $\mu$ satisfies $$1\le\mu(e^L),\qquad
 \mu(e^{2L})\le e^{C\zeta^2},\qquad
 \mu((e^L-1)^2)\le C'\zeta^2.$$ Thus fixed-cost posterior means and second moments change by $O(\zeta)$, using their fourth moments. Large tilts are removed by polynomial truncation. In the size-biased exploration used below, the precise bound for fixed boxes $B,i$ is $$\label{eq:profile-large-tilts}
 \sum_{\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}C\ge r}M_C(B)\mathbf 1_{\{\zeta_C>1\}}
       (1+h_i^2+M_C(i)^2)
       \le \sqrt{Q_BE_2}\,(1+h_i^2+Q_i).$$ For $\zeta_C\le1$, the corresponding errors are bounded by $\sqrt{Q_B}(1+\sqrt{Q_i})(\sqrt{E_2}+E_2)$. Hölder, the weighted moments, and <a href="#eq:profile-tilt-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-tilt-moments">[eq:profile-tilt-moments]</a> make both expectations vanish at fixed $\lambda$ after the mesh and cell limits. For exploration from a filled region the same conclusion follows directly by discarding $\zeta>1$ using <a href="#eq:profile-tilt-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-tilt-moments">[eq:profile-tilt-moments]</a> and the actual band moments. The finite tilt estimates used here are <a id="pm-cite-69"></a>[[X](#ref-X), Equations (3.12)–(3.14)]; the argument above identifies the sequence limits of all their moment inputs.

*Vanishing reach.* It remains to show that exploration from $p$ is unlikely to cross the band and reach $V$. Choose finite small-cell coverings $i$ and $B$ on the starting and target sides, respectively, with fixed positive gaps from the band, the starting region, and the target region. They cover separator strips, so any crossing cluster meets a cell on each side. The radius $\rho$ of these cells is fixed before the band refinement. Choose the macroscopic threshold $r>0$ below the fixed separations that a cluster joining the starting region, either comparison strip, the band, or the target must span. Every cluster contributing to the cross terms below then has diameter at least $r$, and the previous moment and tilt estimates apply with this one threshold. Path anchoring bounds the remaining centered variance of an average in a reached cell by a constant $C_0$ independent of $\rho$. On the other hand, a logarithmic trial function supported away from the band gives the continuum band variance lower bound $$c a_*\log(1/\rho)-C.$$ The trial pays no band mass, so this bound is uniform in $\lambda$. At a free wall it is restricted to the rectangle, which is permitted for the Neumann form. Since $a_*>0$, choose $\rho$ so small that the prior variance exceeds $C_0+2c_0$ for a fixed $c_0>0$. The band limits then give a gap at least $c_0$ in the discrete laws, for each fixed strength and sufficiently fine partition and mesh. This is the only step that requires a positive height coefficient; it uses $a_*>0$ in the limiting band law. The finite estimates require only $b\in J$, with no assumption on $a(b_k)$.

<a id="eq:profile-exterior-deficit"></a>
<a id="eq:profile-biased-deficit"></a>
There are two useful consequences of this gap. Write a target average as exposed plus unvisited height. The centered posterior variance is at most the prior variance and loses at least $c_0$ if the cell is reached. Restoring its tilt has the vanishing errors just proved. Fair-sign resampling of the exposed term gives $$\label{eq:profile-exterior-deficit}
 c_0\mathbb P_{W,N}(B\text{ reached})
    \le\mathbb E_{W,N}\sum_{C:C\cap p\ne\varnothing}M_C(B)^2+o(1).$$ Here and below $o(1)$ vanishes in the first two limits at fixed $\lambda$. A second comparison starts by biasing with $|h_x|$, for $x\in B$, and exposing its cluster. The finite size-bias inequality $$\mathbb E[X^2|Y|]\ge\mathbb EX^2\,\mathbb E|Y|$$ holds for real linear tests under centered band precision. It follows from the minimal characteristic logarithmic Hessian in the finite comparison rules: integrate $\mathbb E[X^2\cos(tY)]\le\mathbb EX^2\,\mathbb E\cos(tY)$ against $2\,dt/(\pi t^2)$ after subtracting the identity at zero. Consequently the pre-exposure second moment at $i$ is at least its unbiased variance. The completed-cluster bias cancels from the conditional complement law. Apply the same variance deficit, multiply by $\mathbb E|h_x|$, and average $x$ over $B$. This yields $$\label{eq:profile-biased-deficit}
 c_0\mathbb E_{W,N}\sum_{C:C\cap i\ne\varnothing}M_C(B)
       \le\mathbb E_{W,N}\sum_C M_C(B)M_C(i)^2+o(1).$$ Only clusters spanning the fixed separation contribute to the exposed or tilted terms. They have a fixed positive diameter, so <a href="#eq:profile-large-tilts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-large-tilts">[eq:profile-large-tilts]</a> justifies their summed errors. Equations <a href="#eq:profile-exterior-deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-exterior-deficit">[eq:profile-exterior-deficit]</a> and <a href="#eq:profile-biased-deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-biased-deficit">[eq:profile-biased-deficit]</a> are the two finite deficit comparisons in <a id="pm-cite-70"></a>[[X](#ref-X), Equations (3.16)–(3.17), proof of Proposition 3.8].

<a id="eq:profile-vanishing-reach"></a>
As $\lambda\to\infty$, the inverse forms $T_\lambda^{-1}$ decrease to the inverse with a hard zero band on $K$. This follows from weak energy compactness and recovery by functions zero on $K$. The band separates $B$ from $i$, so $(B,T_\lambda^{-1}i)\to0$. By <a href="#eq:profile-cluster-cross" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-cluster-cross">[eq:profile-cluster-cross]</a>, the limiting expectation of $\sum_CM_C(B)M_C(i)$ therefore vanishes. On $\max_CM_C(i)\le R$, the right side of <a href="#eq:profile-biased-deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-biased-deficit">[eq:profile-biased-deficit]</a> is at most $R$ times this cross sum. Its remaining tail tends to zero as $R\to\infty$, uniformly after the first two limits and toward large $\lambda$, because $$\sum_CM_C(B)M_C(i)^2\le\sqrt{Q_B}\,Q_i$$ and the $Q$’s have arbitrary fixed moments uniformly in that order of limits. Thus the first-mass sum on the left of <a href="#eq:profile-biased-deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-biased-deficit">[eq:profile-biased-deficit]</a> vanishes. Every cluster joining $p$ to $B$ visits some cell $i$. Truncating $\max_CM_C(B)$ in <a href="#eq:profile-exterior-deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-exterior-deficit">[eq:profile-exterior-deficit]</a> and applying these first-mass bounds, with the same moment control of its tail, proves $$\label{eq:profile-vanishing-reach}
 \lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\limsup_{\ell\downarrow0}
 \limsup_{k\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}
 \mathbb P_{W,N}\bigl(\text{exploration from }p
                     \text{ reaches }V\bigr)=0.$$

*Pinned tests and the grounded approximation.* On a finite subdivided graph, explore every cluster meeting the primary pin region. If it does not reach $V$, the exposed part of $X_{g,n}$ is zero. The centered posterior has the pin $p$, possibly further zero pins and increased segment precision, and a nonnegative restricted band form. Its variance is at most the variance of $X_{g,n}$ under the primary hard-pin law on that subdivided graph, by finite precision comparison. At fixed primary graph, this latter variance converges as $N\to\infty$ to the Bessel hard-pin variance. The averaged tilt errors vanish as above. On failure, Cauchy–Schwarz bounds the actual second-moment contribution by the fourth-moment root times the square root of the failure probability. The band fourth moment is uniformly bounded in the successive limits, whereas <a href="#eq:profile-vanishing-reach" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-vanishing-reach">[eq:profile-vanishing-reach]</a> makes that probability vanish. Hence $$\liminf_{k\to\infty}\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_p X_{g,n}
                   \ge a_*\mathcal E_U(g,g).$$ The free variance bound gives the opposite limsup. For every real $t$, the finite lower bound for the logarithmic moment generating function and the upper bound obtained by releasing the pin give $$\frac{t^2}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_p X_{g,n}
       \le \log\mathbb E_p e^{tX_{g,n}}
       \le \log\mathbb E_{\rm free}e^{tX_{g,n}}.$$ The free release is permitted because $A_{U,n}g_n$ has total zero. The two endpoints converge to $a_*t^2\mathcal E_U(g,g)/2$. Applying this to finite linear combinations gives the joint Gaussian Laplace limit for these tests; uniform primary exponential moments give their polynomial moment limits. Weak free-face terms may be approximated first by bounded density smears in primary reference cost, with the gap from $p$ and $K$ preserved.

<a id="eq:profile-grounded-approximation"></a>
Finally let $f$ be any bounded piecewise continuous density, and set $v=G_pf$. Smooth shifts $g_j$ vanishing near $p$, and allowed to meet the free walls, are dense in $\mathcal H_p$ in energy. Choose $\mathcal E_U(v-g_j,v-g_j)\to0$. The ordinary mixed-form approximation on these rectangular geometries, as stated in <a id="pm-cite-71"></a>[[X](#ref-X), Section 3.1 and proof of Theorem 3.1], gives $$\label{eq:profile-grounded-approximation}
 \lim_{k\to\infty}
 \mathcal R_{p,n}\bigl(f_n-A_{U,n}g_{j,n}\bigr)
                    =\mathcal E_U(v-g_j,v-g_j),$$ where $\mathcal R_{p,n}$ is the squared dual norm of the unit-edge form grounded on $p$. To see the precise form of this approximation, let $A_{p,n}$ be the restriction to unpinned coordinates and put $v_n=A_{p,n}^{-1}f_n$. The left side before its limit is $$(f_n,A_{p,n}^{-1}f_n)-2(f_n,g_{j,n})
                +(g_{j,n},A_{U,n}g_{j,n}).$$ Mixed inverse-form convergence, quadrature, and smooth energy consistency give respectively $\mathcal E_U(v,v)$, $\mathcal E_U(v,g_j)$, and $\mathcal E_U(g_j,g_j)$, proving <a href="#eq:profile-grounded-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-grounded-approximation">[eq:profile-grounded-approximation]</a>. Values of a test on pinned vertices are immaterial in this norm. Equivalently, a flow representing its unpinned coefficients may discharge into the pinned vertices. This is why <a href="#eq:profile-grounded-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-grounded-approximation">[eq:profile-grounded-approximation]</a> imposes no zero-total condition on $f$.

Uniform reference-cost bounds make the difference between the two smears in <a href="#eq:profile-grounded-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-grounded-approximation">[eq:profile-grounded-approximation]</a> tend to zero in every fixed moment, first as $k\to\infty$ and then $j\to\infty$. Their fixed linear exponential bounds give the same passage for Laplace transforms, by Cauchy–Schwarz applied to $e^{t(h,f_n)}-e^{tX_{g_j,n}}$. The limiting variance is $a_*\mathcal E_U(v,v)=a_*\int_UfG_pf$. Apply the argument to every finite linear combination of the $f_i$’s and use polarization to obtain <a href="#eq:profile-pinned-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-pinned-covariance">[eq:profile-pinned-covariance]</a>, with the asserted joint Laplace and moment convergence. ◻

</details>

Two choices of pin will be used below. If $D$ is an inner rectangle and $p=U\setminus D$ is its positive-width rectangular rim, then $\mathcal H_p$ identifies with $H^1_0(D)$ by zero extension, and <a href="#eq:profile-pinned-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-pinned-covariance">[eq:profile-pinned-covariance]</a> is the Dirichlet covariance $a_*G_D$. If a positive-width primary collar surrounds a compactly supported shift inside an unwrapped torus rectangle, zero pinning that collar separates its interior law from the exterior. The lemma therefore applies to that interior law as well.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Torus limits and uniform zero-coefficient estimates

<a id="stmt-prop-gaussian-profile"></a>
<div id="prop:gaussian-profile" class="proposition">

**Proposition 21** (Gaussian laws at continuity scales). *Let $w$ be a continuity point of the extracted profile, let $\delta_k\log_Ln_k\to w$, and put $a_*=\mathcal A(w)$. The free rectangular limits are those in Lemma <a href="#lem:profile-free" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:profile-free">18</a>; if $a_*>0$, their fractional means have the conclusion of Lemma <a href="#lem:profile-phase" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:profile-phase">19</a>, and their fixed primary pinned geometries have the Gaussian Laplace and moment limits of Lemma <a href="#lem:profile-pins" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:profile-pins">20</a>. In particular, on a square interior surrounded by a fixed positive-width zero rim, the covariance of bounded piecewise continuous density smears converges to $a_*(-\Delta_D)^{-1}$, with Dirichlet boundary on that square.*

<a id="eq:profile-torus"></a>
*On the zero-winding height torus of side $n_k$, every smooth periodic shift $g$ satisfies $$\label{eq:profile-torus}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(h,A_{\mathrm{tor}}g_k)\longrightarrow
                         a_*\int_{\mathbb T^2}|\nabla g|^2.$$ Thus the area-weighted first cosine observation in <a href="#eq:physical-cosine" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-cosine">[eq:physical-cosine]</a> has limiting variance $a_*\mathfrak g$, where $\mathfrak g=1/(8\pi^2)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-gaussian-profile"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-gaussian-profile</summary>

*Proof.* The free and pinned assertions have been proved. For the torus upper bound, cut into small fixed rectangles, restrict the gradient test to each piece, and use <a href="#eq:profile-partial-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-partial-upper">[eq:profile-partial-upper]</a>. The omitted seams and the gradient approximations have uniformly vanishing reference cost. Therefore the limsup in <a href="#eq:profile-torus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-torus">[eq:profile-torus]</a> is at most its right side. This already proves the assertion when $a_*=0$.

For $a_*>0$, first suppose that $g$ is supported in an unwrapped rectangle strictly inside one torus chart. Add primary zero pins on a collar of fixed positive width surrounding its support inside that chart. Centered precision comparison decreases variance. The collar separates the interior law from the torus exterior, and Lemma <a href="#lem:profile-pins" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:profile-pins">20</a> gives the interior variance limit. Indeed the torus test equals the grounded interior test there, and $(-\Delta_D)^{-1}(-\Delta g)=g$ because $g$ vanishes near the collar. Its variance is therefore $a_*\int|\nabla g|^2$, matching the upper bound. For general $g$, use a finite smooth partition of unity to write $g$ as a sum of shifts supported in such charts. On each joint covariance subsequence the upper-bound defect is a positive semidefinite quadratic form. Since its diagonal vanishes on each summand, its cross terms with those summands vanish as well. It therefore vanishes on their sum.

For the cosine density $f(x)=\cos(2\pi x_1)$, its inverse torus Laplacian is $g=f/(4\pi^2)$ and $\int|\nabla g|^2=1/(8\pi^2)$. The discrete eigenvalue converges to $4\pi^2$ after multiplication by $n_k^2$. The normalizing correction has vanishing reference cost, which proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-profile-zero-uniform"></a>
<div id="cor:profile-zero-uniform" class="corollary">

**Corollary 22** (Uniform affine transforms on a zero interval). *Suppose $I=[u,v]\subset(0,\infty)$ consists of continuity points of $\mathcal A$ and $\mathcal A=0$ on $I$. Fix a compact set $K\subset\mathbb R^2$ and $\lambda\in\mathbb R$. For a free square of side $m$ write $X_{m,p}=(h,A_{B_m}(p\cdot x/m))$. Then <a id="eq:profile-zero-uniform"></a>$$\label{eq:profile-zero-uniform}
 \sup_{\substack{m\in\mathbb N:\ \delta_k\log_Lm\in I\\p\in K}}
 \left|\mathbb E_{B_m,b_k}^{\mathrm{free}}e^{\lambda X_{m,p}}-1\right|
                                      \longrightarrow0.$$ The assertion is unchanged by translating the squares or multiplying their sides by factors in a fixed compact subset of $(0,\infty)$, provided their resulting logarithmic scales stay in a fixed zero-profile neighborhood of $I$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-profile-zero-uniform"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-profile-zero-uniform</summary>

*Proof.* If $a_m(b_k)$ failed to tend to zero uniformly on the stated set, there would be offending $m_k$ and a subsequence with $\delta_k\log_Lm_k\to w\in I$. This contradicts <a href="#eq:profile-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-limit">[eq:profile-limit]</a>. Square symmetry gives $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}X_{m,p}=a_m(b_k)|p|^2$, uniformly small for $p\in K$. The reference exponential bound at $\pm2\lambda$ supplies uniform integrability of $e^{\lambda X_{m,p}}$. Since $X_{m,p}\to0$ in probability uniformly over these choices, the expectations tend to one uniformly. Translation does not change the law. The extra side factors change $\delta_k\log_Lm$ by $o(1)$; compactness gives the last statement. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Excluding intermediate positive coefficients

The fixed-temperature coefficient gap in <a id="pm-cite-72"></a>[[X](#ref-X), Theorem 2.5] is proved by growth of a face characteristic coefficient. For a moving temperature, a single growth comparison cannot be iterated at arbitrarily many scales. We instead integrate those comparisons over a compact logarithmic interval. The initial characteristic coefficient must therefore have a quantitative lower bound on this interval.

Let $\rho_m^{\mathrm{sym}}$ be one quarter of the sum of the four face probabilities of $B_m$, and define <a id="eq:profile-z"></a>$$\label{eq:profile-z}
 z_m(b)=\mathbb E_{B_m,b}^{\mathrm{free}}
                           e^{i(h,\rho_m^{\mathrm{sym}})}.$$ It is strictly positive and at most one.

<a id="stmt-lem-profile-ratio"></a>
<div id="lem:profile-ratio" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:profile-ratio"></a>
<a id="eq:profile-ratio-rough"></a>
**Lemma 23** (Relative growth of the face phase). *There is $c>0$, independent of sufficiently large fixed integer $G$, with the following property. If $w$ is a continuity point and $\mathcal A(w)=a_*>0$, then along every sequence $\delta_k\log_Lm_k\to w$, $$\label{eq:profile-ratio}
 \liminf_k\frac{z_{Gm_k}(b_k)}{z_{m_k}(b_k)}
                          \ge cG^{2-a_* /(4\pi)}.$$ For each fixed such $G$ there is also $c(G)>0$ such that $$\label{eq:profile-ratio-rough}
                     z_{Gm}(b)/z_m(b)\ge c(G)$$ for all sufficiently large $m$ and all $b$ in the fixed compact temperature interval.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-profile-ratio"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-profile-ratio</summary>

*Proof.* We use the finite soft joining formula <a id="pm-cite-73"></a>[[X](#ref-X), Equation (2.11)]. Tile $B_{Gm}$ by $G^2$ free $m$-squares. The outer-face phase has cell totals $\xi_i$, supported on boundary cells, with $\sum_i\xi_i=1$ and $\sum_i\xi_i^2\le C/G$. On the space of neutral cell totals choose a flow right inverse $P$ on the neighboring-cell graph, and let $\rho_{ij}$ be the probability on the face of cell $i$ adjoining cell $j$. Define a vector $\mathsf H$ in that neutral Euclidean space by $$(\mathsf H,d)=\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}P(d)_{ij}
       \bigl((h_i,\rho_{ij})-(h_j,\rho_{ji})
                         -\bar\eta_{ij}\bigr),$$ where $\bar\eta_{ij}$ is the mean of the independent Bessel seam increments with the same orientation. Translating cells by constants changes $\mathsf H$ by the neutral projection of those constants. Penalize by $e^{-|\mathsf H|^2/2}$ and sum relative translations, quotienting by their common translation. The auxiliary-confinement argument in the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:profile-phase" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:profile-phase">19</a> justifies the finite precision comparisons for these sums. Poisson summation gives the positive numerator <a id="eq:profile-soft-sum"></a>$$\label{eq:profile-soft-sum}
 \sum_{\substack{q_i\in\mathbb Z\\\sum_iq_i=1}}
 e^{-|q-\xi|^2/2}\,
 \prod_i\phi_{m,b}\left(s_i+
              \sum_{j\sim i}P(q-\xi)_{ij}\rho_{ij}\right)
 \chi_m(P(q-\xi)),$$ up to a common scalar. Here $s_i$ is the restriction of the outer phase to cell $i$, $\rho_{ij}$ is its seam-face probability, and $\chi_m$ is the characteristic function of the independent seam averages. The denominator is the corresponding sum with $s=0$ and total zero. This follows by Fourier transforming the Gaussian penalty on the neutral space. The reciprocal of the projected cell-translation lattice is the neutral integer lattice. Exact seam matching adds centered precision, so the joined coefficient is at least this soft coefficient.

For fixed $G$, the denominator tends to one along the sequence in <a href="#eq:profile-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-ratio">[eq:profile-ratio]</a>. Its zero sector is one. Every nonzero sector contains a nonzero integer cell total and tends to zero by Lemma <a href="#lem:profile-phase" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:profile-phase">19</a>; the Gaussian weights permit dominated convergence. Each fixed-flux seam characteristic tends to one because the seam averages have variance $O(m^{-1})$.

<a id="eq:profile-radial-cost"></a>
Retain the sectors $q=e_{i_0}$ with $i_0$ at distance at least $G/4$ from the boundary. The radial flow of <a id="pm-cite-74"></a>[[X](#ref-X), Equation (2.14)] has outward face fluxes $H_{i,\pm j}$, constant outer flux $1/(4G)$, and cell divergence $e_{i_0}$. Set $$D_{ij}=\tfrac12(H_{i,+j}-H_{i,-j}),\qquad
 S_{ij}=H_{i,+j}+H_{i,-j}.$$ The unit-cell radial field, corrected to have constant boundary flux, gives $$\label{eq:profile-radial-cost}
 \sum_{i,j}D_{ij}^2\le\frac1{2\pi}\log G+C,
 \qquad \sum_{i,j}S_{ij}^2\le C.$$ One may obtain it by integrating $(x-x_0)/(2\pi|x-x_0|^2)+E(x)$ across cell faces, where the Neumann boundary correction has $|E|\le C/G$ and $|\nabla E|\le C/G^2$. The radial energy is $(2\pi)^{-1}\log G+O(1)$, and the face-sum errors are summable. The constants are uniform for the retained central cells. Prescribe $P(e_{i_0}-\xi)$ to be these flows for all retained $i_0$ and extend linearly. This is possible because the vectors $e_{i_0}-\xi$ are linearly independent, their central coordinates being distinct.

<a id="eq:profile-relative-lower"></a>
In each cell expand the logarithm of its characteristic coefficient about the symmetric four-face profile of the same total. Square symmetry makes the linear term vanish on every zero-total face perturbation. The logarithmic Hessian bound thus gives the product lower bound $$\label{eq:profile-relative-lower}
        z_m(b)\exp\left\{-\tfrac12\sum_i\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(h,Y_i)\right\},$$ where $Y_i$ is the difference from that symmetric profile. Since $q_i=S_{i1}+S_{i2}$, its decomposition is explicitly $$\begin{aligned}
 Y_i={}&\sum_{j=1}^2D_{ij}(\rho_{i,+j}-\rho_{i,-j})\\
 &+\frac{S_{i1}-S_{i2}}4
   (\rho_{i,+1}+\rho_{i,-1}-\rho_{i,+2}-\rho_{i,-2}).
\end{aligned}$$ Square reflections make these three components mutually orthogonal. The two odd face differences have variance exactly $a_m(b)$; the even component has reference cost at most $C(S_{i1}^2+S_{i2}^2)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:profile-free" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:profile-free">18</a> and the reference bound give $$\limsup_k\sum_i\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(h,Y_i)
       \le a_*\sum_{i,j}D_{ij}^2+C\sum_{i,j}S_{ij}^2.$$ The Gaussian sector weight is bounded below because $|e_{i_0}-\xi|$ is bounded. There are at least $c_0G^2$ such sectors. Combining these facts with <a href="#eq:profile-radial-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-radial-cost">[eq:profile-radial-cost]</a> proves <a href="#eq:profile-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-ratio">[eq:profile-ratio]</a>. The additive energy constant and the even component constant are uniform, since $a_*\le a$ and the reference bounds are uniform. Therefore its prefactor $c$ does not depend on $G$ or on the positive continuity value.

For <a href="#eq:profile-ratio-rough" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-ratio-rough">[eq:profile-ratio-rough]</a>, keep $G$ fixed and retain just one central sector. Bound every variance in <a href="#eq:profile-relative-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-relative-lower">[eq:profile-relative-lower]</a> by its reference cost. The soft denominator is at most the fixed finite-dimensional Gaussian lattice sum, since all its characteristic factors are at most one. The selected seam factor has a uniform positive lower bound: a mean over $m$ independent Bessel increments at fixed flux $q$ has characteristic function $\exp\{bm(\cos(2\pi q/m)-1)\}$, which is bounded below uniformly in large $m$ and in the compact temperature interval. There are only finitely many seams. These bounds prove <a href="#eq:profile-ratio-rough" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-ratio-rough">[eq:profile-ratio-rough]</a>. Crucially, <a href="#eq:profile-relative-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-relative-lower">[eq:profile-relative-lower]</a> retains the factor $z_m(b)$ regardless of how small it is. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-profile-subexponential"></a>
<div id="lem:profile-subexponential" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:profile-subexponential"></a>
**Lemma 24** (Critical lower bound for the face phase). *For the fixed buffer $D_0$ and block ratio $L$, $$\label{eq:profile-subexponential}
 -\log z_{D_0L^j}(b_c)=o(j),\qquad j\to\infty.$$ Moreover $z_m(b)\ge z_m(b_c)$ whenever $b\le b_c$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-profile-subexponential"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-profile-subexponential</summary>

*Proof.* At $b_c$, <a id="pm-cite-75"></a>[[X](#ref-X), Equation (2.15)], the fixed-temperature version of <a href="#eq:profile-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-ratio">[eq:profile-ratio]</a>, has coefficient $a=8\pi$. Taking $c_1$ to be the smaller of $1$ and half the prefactor in that liminf bound, we obtain a constant independent of sufficiently large fixed $r$ such that for $G=L^r$, $$z_{Gm}(b_c)\ge c_1z_m(b_c)$$ for all $m$ beyond a threshold depending on $r$. Fix $r$ and iterate on each residue class of $j$ modulo $r$. The finitely many initial coefficients are strictly positive. Hence $$\liminf_{j\to\infty}j^{-1}\log z_{D_0L^j}(b_c)
                                      \ge r^{-1}\log c_1.$$ Now let $r\to\infty$ and use $z_m\le1$ to obtain <a href="#eq:profile-subexponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-subexponential">[eq:profile-subexponential]</a>. Characteristic coefficients decrease with $b$ by the chain precision comparison, giving the last claim. The lower bound is therefore uniform for all $b\le b_c$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-profile-step"></a>
<div id="prop:profile-step" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:profile-step"></a>
**Proposition 25** (The profile has one jump, at the passage time). *The profile extracted in Proposition <a href="#prop:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:profile">17</a> satisfies $$\label{eq:profile-step}
 \mathcal A(w)=a\quad(0<w<T),\qquad
 \mathcal A(w)=0\quad(w>T),
 \qquad T=\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{3\mathcal P}}.$$ In particular all points of these two open intervals are continuity points. No assertion about its value at the single point $T$ is needed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-profile-step"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-profile-step</summary>

*Proof.* First we exclude a continuity value in $(0,a)$. If such a value exists, monotonicity and continuity give a compact interval $[u,v]\subset(0,\infty)$ of positive length and $\eta>0$ such that all its continuity values lie in $[\eta,a-\eta]$. Choose a fixed integer stride $r$ so large that, for $G=L^r$, $cG^{2-(a-\eta)/(4\pi)}>2$, where $c$ is from Lemma <a href="#lem:profile-ratio" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:profile-ratio">23</a>.

<a id="eq:profile-fatou"></a>
On one residue class modulo $r$ take consecutive indices $j$ for which $\delta_k j$ runs across $[u,v]$, and define on their cells of width $\delta_kr$ the step function $$F_k(t)=\log\frac{z_{D_0L^{j+r}}(b_k)}{z_{D_0L^j}(b_k)}.$$ Choose the endpoints of this union of cells to converge to $u,v$. At almost every $t\in(u,v)$ the profile is continuous. The starting cell size has logarithmic scale tending to $t$, so <a href="#eq:profile-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-ratio">[eq:profile-ratio]</a> gives $\liminf_k F_k(t)\ge\log2$. Equation <a href="#eq:profile-ratio-rough" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-ratio-rough">[eq:profile-ratio-rough]</a> gives the common lower bound $F_k\ge\log c(G)$. Extend by zero outside the chosen cells in a fixed enclosing interval. Fatou’s lemma therefore gives $$\label{eq:profile-fatou}
          \liminf_k\int F_k(t)\,dt\ge(v-u)\log2>0.$$ On the other hand, the integral telescopes exactly: $$\int F_k(t)\,dt
 =\delta_kr\bigl[\log z_{D_0L^{j_+(k)}}(b_k)
                    -\log z_{D_0L^{j_-(k)}}(b_k)\bigr].$$ Since $z\le1$, Lemma <a href="#lem:profile-subexponential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:profile-subexponential">24</a> bounds its limsup by $\limsup_k\delta_kr\,o(j_-(k))=0$; here $\delta_kj_-(k)\to u>0$. This contradicts <a href="#eq:profile-fatou" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-fatou">[eq:profile-fatou]</a>. Thus every continuity value belongs to $\{0,a\}$.

For $0<w<T$, Proposition <a href="#prop:passage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:passage">16</a> gives the torus cosine variance limit $a\mathfrak g$, whereas Proposition <a href="#prop:gaussian-profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gaussian-profile">21</a> gives $\mathcal A(w)\mathfrak g$ at continuity points. Thus $\mathcal A(w)=a$ at all such points. Suppose a continuity point $w_0>T$ also had value $a$. Monotonicity and the bound $\mathcal A\le a$ would make the profile identically $a$ on a neighborhood of $T$, so $T$ would be a continuity point. The moving exit sizes $n_{j_e}=D_0L^{j_e}$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:passage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:passage">16</a> have $\delta_kj_e\to T$. Proposition <a href="#prop:gaussian-profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gaussian-profile">21</a> would give cosine variance tending to $a\mathfrak g$ there, contradicting the strictly smaller exit limsup. Every continuity point above $T$ therefore has value zero. Finally a monotone function whose values on the continuity points of an open interval are a single constant is that constant everywhere on the interval. This proves <a href="#eq:profile-step" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-step">[eq:profile-step]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:mass"></a>
### From the height profile to the spin mass

The height profile identifies a transition at the logarithmic scale $T$. We now show that the correlation length has this same scale. Positive pinned-height covariance below $T$ prevents a substantially shorter correlation length. Above $T$, small free-height fluctuations allow a circulation estimate with an arbitrarily large fixed tilt; a finite-size correlation inequality then gives the opposite bound. Both arguments concern the free-box thermodynamic correlation used in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="eq:mass-profile"></a>
<a id="eq:mass-scale-goal"></a>
Throughout this section, fix a sequence $b_k< b_c$ tending to $b_c$, put $\delta_k=\sqrt{b_c-b_k}$, and pass to a subsequence furnished by Proposition <a href="#prop:profile-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:profile-step">25</a>. On this subsequence the height profile satisfies $$\label{eq:mass-profile}
 \mathcal A(w)=a\quad(0<w<T),\qquad
 \mathcal A(w)=0\quad(w>T),\qquad a=8\pi.$$ All finite-graph estimates below are uniform when $b$ belongs to a fixed compact interval $I\subset(0,\infty)$ containing the tail of $(b_k)$. We will prove $$\label{eq:mass-scale-goal}
             \delta_k\log_L\frac1{m(b_k)}\longrightarrow T.$$

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Correlation inequalities with the prescribed mass

For a finite graph $G$, write $$C_{G,b}(u,v)=\left\langle e^{i(\theta_u-\theta_v)}\right\rangle_{G,b},
 \qquad
 C^{(2)}_{G,b}(u,v)=
 \left\langle e^{2i(\theta_u-\theta_v)}\right\rangle_{G,b}.$$ Here every edge has the cosine interaction with parameter $b$, and the boundary is free. Reflection of all angles makes these quantities real, and their Fourier expansions make them nonnegative. We use $C_b(x)$ for the infinite-volume free correlation between $0$ and $x\in\mathbb Z^2$, so $C_b(r)=C_b(re_1)$.

<a id="stmt-lem-mass-correlation-bounds"></a>
<div id="lem:mass-correlation-bounds" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:mass-pointwise"></a>
<a id="eq:mass-charge-two"></a>
**Lemma 26**. *For every $b>0$ and $x\in\mathbb Z^2$, $$\label{eq:mass-pointwise}
                       C_b(x)\le e^{-m(b)\|x\|_\infty}.$$ If $G$ is a finite subgraph of $\mathbb Z^2$, then $$\label{eq:mass-charge-two}
 0\le C^{(2)}_{G,b}(u,v)\le4C_{G,b}(u,v)
                         \le4C_b(v-u).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-mass-correlation-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-mass-correlation-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* The Ginibre inequalities <a id="pm-cite-76"></a>[[Ginibre](#ref-Ginibre)] imply positive correlation for cosine characters and monotonicity of two-point correlations under increasing ferromagnetic edge couplings. In particular, finite free correlations are bounded above by their free-exhaustion limits; see also <a id="pm-cite-77"></a>[[vEL](#ref-vEL), Lemma 16]. These limits are translation invariant: translating any finite graph gives another finite graph in the same monotone exhaustion.

We first record supermultiplicativity. For three vertices $u,v,z$, Ginibre’s inequality gives $$\left\langle
 \cos(\theta_u-\theta_v)\cos(\theta_z-\theta_v)
 \right\rangle
 \ge C_{G,b}(u,v)C_{G,b}(z,v).$$ The expectation of $\sin(\theta_u-\theta_v)\sin(\theta_z-\theta_v)$ is nonnegative. To see this, condition on $\theta_v$, rotate it to zero, and then condition on all cosine coordinates. Writing a sine coordinate as its absolute value times its sign, the remaining signs have a ferromagnetic zero-field Ising distribution: the sign coupling on an edge $\{x,y\}$ is $b|\sin\theta_x\sin\theta_y|\ge0$. The sign at $v$, whose sine is zero, is irrelevant. Their two-point sign expectations are therefore nonnegative. The cosine addition formula now proves $$C_{G,b}(u,z)\ge C_{G,b}(u,v)C_{G,b}(v,z).$$ After free exhaustion this becomes $C_b(x+y)\ge C_b(x)C_b(y)$. For every positive integer $n$, $C_b(jne_1)\ge C_b(ne_1)^j$. Taking $j\to\infty$ in the definition of the axis mass yields $C_b(ne_1)\le e^{-m(b)n}$. If $x=(p,q)$ and $|p|\ge|q|$, reflection symmetry and supermultiplicativity give $$C_b(x)^2=C_b(p,q)C_b(p,-q)\le C_b(2p,0)
                                      \le e^{-2m(b)|p|}.$$ Interchanging coordinates treats the other case and proves <a href="#eq:mass-pointwise" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-pointwise">[eq:mass-pointwise]</a> with the mass specified in the statement of the main theorem.

For the charge-two estimate, fix an axis and abbreviate $s_x=\sin\theta_x$, $c_x=\cos\theta_x$. Rotation invariance gives $$C_{G,b}(u,v)=2\langle s_us_v\rangle_{G,b},\qquad
 C^{(2)}_{G,b}(u,v)=8\langle c_uc_vs_us_v\rangle_{G,b}.$$ The same conditional sign argument gives $\langle s_us_v\mid(c_x)_{x\in G}\rangle\ge0$. Since $c_uc_v\le1$, the second identity is at most $8\langle s_us_v\rangle_{G,b}=4C_{G,b}(u,v)$. Edge monotonicity finishes <a href="#eq:mass-charge-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-charge-two">[eq:mass-charge-two]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Pinned covariance gives a lower bound on length

<a id="eq:mass-current-weight"></a>
<a id="eq:mass-loop-weight"></a>
We use the random-loop representation of the XY current due to van Engelenburg and Lis <a id="pm-cite-78"></a>[[vEL](#ref-vEL), Section 3]. We describe its normalization because the height boundary condition and the loop orientation are important here. For each oriented edge of a finite graph $G$, let $n_{uv}\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}$ be a current. A current with zero divergence at every vertex has weight $$\label{eq:mass-current-weight}
            \prod_{\{u,v\}\in E(G)}
       \frac{(b/2)^{n_{uv}+n_{vu}}}{n_{uv}!\,n_{vu}!}.$$ Equivalently, take independent Poisson variables of mean $b/2$ on the oriented edges and condition on zero divergence. For an unoriented edge put $N_e=n_{uv}+n_{vu}$, label its $N_e$ copies, and assign an orientation to each copy. Each labeled assignment receives edge weight $(b/2)^{N_e}/N_e!$. At a vertex with $\ell_v$ incoming and $\ell_v$ outgoing half-edges, pair them by a uniformly chosen bijection. The resulting directed loops have weight $$\label{eq:mass-loop-weight}
 \prod_{e\in E(G)}\frac{(b/2)^{N_e}}{N_e!}
                     \prod_{v\in V(G)}\frac1{\ell_v!}.$$ Indeed, summing orientations introduces the binomial factor $N_e!/(n_{uv}!n_{vu}!)$, and summing pairings cancels the vertex factor. Thus this is a probability extension of the normalized current law <a href="#eq:mass-current-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-current-weight">[eq:mass-current-weight]</a>.

<a id="eq:mass-winding-height"></a>
Let $\mathbf P_{G,b}$ denote this loop law. Given the undirected paired loops, their orientations are independent and fair. The reason is that $\ell_v=\deg(v)/2$ is fixed by that paired geometry, so every choice of loop orientations has the same weight in <a href="#eq:mass-loop-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-loop-weight">[eq:mass-loop-weight]</a>. For a planar rectangle, current integration with exterior face height zero gives $$\label{eq:mass-winding-height}
                  h(z)=2\pi\sum_\gamma W_\gamma(z),$$ where $W_\gamma(z)$ is the oriented winding number of the loop $\gamma$ around the bounded face $z$ <a id="pm-cite-79"></a>[[vEL](#ref-vEL), Proposition 7]. The factor $2\pi$ accounts for our height spacing.

<a id="eq:mass-loop-connection"></a>
We will also use the following consequence of the loop switching identity <a id="pm-cite-80"></a>[[vEL](#ref-vEL), Lemma 8]: $$\label{eq:mass-loop-connection}
 \mathbf P_{G,b}\{\text{a loop visits both }u\text{ and }v\}
                         \le2C^{(2)}_{G,b}(u,v).$$ In the notation of that lemma, let $m_{uv}$ be the number of directed pieces of loops from $u$ to $v$ with no internal visit to either endpoint. A loop visiting both endpoints implies $m_{uv}\ge1$, and the identity is $C^{(2)}_{G,b}(u,v)=\mathbf E_{G,b}[m_{uv}/(m_{uv}+1)]$. Thus <a href="#eq:mass-loop-connection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-loop-connection">[eq:mass-loop-connection]</a> applies to precisely the normalized loop law above.

<a id="stmt-lem-mass-loop-covariance"></a>
<div id="lem:mass-loop-covariance" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:mass-covariance-decay"></a>
**Lemma 27** (Separated height covariance). *Fix $d,M,c_1>0$ and a compact $I\subset(0,\infty)$. Let $G_n$ be a free spin rectangle contained in a square of side $c_1n$. On its bounded faces take the dual height law with exterior face height zero. Let $$F_n=\sum_z f_{n,z}h(z),\qquad
               H_n=\sum_z g_{n,z}h(z),$$ where $\sum_z|f_{n,z}|,\sum_z|g_{n,z}|\le M$, and the two supports are separated horizontally by at least $dn$. There are constants $c,C>0$, depending only on the displayed fixed parameters, such that, for $b\in I$ and sufficiently large $n$, $$\label{eq:mass-covariance-decay}
 |\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(F_n,H_n)|\le
       Cn^{12}e^{-c n m(b)}+Ce^{-c n^4\log n}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-mass-loop-covariance"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-mass-loop-covariance</summary>

*Proof.* Condition on the undirected paired geometry. Choose one reference orientation for each loop and write its random orientation as $\sigma_\gamma\in\{-1,1\}$. Equation <a href="#eq:mass-winding-height" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-winding-height">[eq:mass-winding-height]</a> gives $$F_n=\sum_\gamma\sigma_\gamma F_n(\gamma),\qquad
 H_n=\sum_\gamma\sigma_\gamma H_n(\gamma),$$ where $F_n(\gamma)=2\pi\sum_zf_{n,z}W_\gamma^{\rm ref}(z)$, and similarly for $H_n(\gamma)$. The conditional means vanish, and the conditional covariance is $\sum_\gamma F_n(\gamma)H_n(\gamma)$. If one summand is nonzero, the loop has nonzero winding around at least one face in each support. Each such face lies in the horizontal range of the loop. Consequently this loop has horizontal diameter at least $dn-O(1)$.

Let $N=\sum_eN_e$ be the total number of traversals, and let $\mathcal B_n$ be the event that some loop has horizontal diameter at least $dn/2$. A loop of length $|\gamma|$ has winding number at most $|\gamma|$ in absolute value. Hence $$\left|\sum_\gamma F_n(\gamma)H_n(\gamma)\right|
 \le (2\pi M)^2\sum_\gamma|\gamma|^2\,\mathbf1_{\mathcal B_n}
 \le (2\pi M)^2N^2\mathbf1_{\mathcal B_n}.$$ There are $O(n^4)$ ordered pairs of vertices in $G_n$. A loop counted by $\mathcal B_n$ visits a pair separated by at least $dn/2$. Using <a href="#eq:mass-loop-connection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-loop-connection">[eq:mass-loop-connection]</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:mass-correlation-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mass-correlation-bounds">26</a>, we obtain $$\mathbf P_{G_n,b}(\mathcal B_n)
 \le C n^4 e^{-c n m(b)}.$$ On $N\le n^4$, the preceding conditional-covariance bound is therefore at most $C n^{12}e^{-c n m(b)}$ after expectation.

It remains to bound the weighted tail of $N$. Before the zero-divergence conditioning, $N$ is Poisson with mean $\mu=b|E(G_n)|\le C_I n^2$. The probability of that conditioning event is at least the probability of the zero configuration, namely $e^{-\mu}$. Thus $$\mathbf E_{G_n,b}[N^2\mathbf1_{\{N>n^4\}}]
 \le e^{\mu}\,
     \mathbb E_{\operatorname{Pois}(\mu)}[N^2\mathbf1_{\{N>n^4\}}]
 \le Ce^{-c n^4\log n}.$$ For the last estimate one may use the Poisson exponential moment with parameter $\log n$: its logarithm is $\mu(n-1)=O(n^3)$, whereas the tail factor is $e^{-n^4\log n}$; the polynomial insertion changes only a polynomial prefactor. Pairings do not change the marginal law of $N$. This proves <a href="#eq:mass-covariance-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-covariance-decay">[eq:mass-covariance-decay]</a>. ◻

</details>

We apply the lemma to the nondegenerate pinned limits supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:gaussian-profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gaussian-profile">21</a>. Let $D=(0,1)^2$, take a larger fixed rectangle $U$, and pin every primary height in a positive-width rim $p=U\setminus D$, with the usual admissible rounding at mesh $n^{-1}$. Choose the unpinned vertices to be exactly the $n$-by-$n$ grid of bounded faces of the spin rectangle $\{0,\ldots,n\}^2$. This is only a translation and an $O(n^{-1})$ rounding of the primary mesh.

The resulting interior height law is exactly the dual law of that *free* spin rectangle. All pin–pin edge factors cancel from the normalized law. Every edge from an unpinned face to the pinned rim becomes an edge to the exterior face; their multiplicities are retained. In particular a corner face has two such exterior edges. There are no fixed boundary spins or additional period constraints. This agrees with free-boundary duality <a id="pm-cite-81"></a>[[X](#ref-X), Lemma 2.1].

<a id="eq:mass-positive-pinned"></a>
Fix smooth nonzero functions $f,g\ge0$, compactly supported in two interior boxes in $D$ with positive horizontal separation, and put $$F_n=n^{-2}\sum_z f(z/n)h(z),\qquad
 H_n=n^{-2}\sum_z g(z/n)h(z).$$ The harmless translations of face centers are included in the argument of the sampled functions. If $\delta_k\log_L n_k\to w\in(0,T)$, Proposition <a href="#prop:gaussian-profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gaussian-profile">21</a> gives $$\label{eq:mass-positive-pinned}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_{b_k,p}(F_{n_k},H_{n_k})
 \longrightarrow a\int_D\int_Df(z)G_D(z,z')g(z')\,dz\,dz'>0,$$ where $G_D$ is the Dirichlet Green kernel on $D$. The strict inequality follows from its positivity in the connected square. These smears have nonzero total mass. Their convergence uses the grounded inverse-form part of Proposition <a href="#prop:gaussian-profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gaussian-profile">21</a>: the primary zero rim fixes the constant, and the approximation cost is the grounded cost on $D$. No mean-zero condition is imposed on $f$ or $g$.

<a id="stmt-prop-mass-lower-length"></a>
<div id="prop:mass-lower-length" class="proposition">

**Proposition 28**. *Along the extracted sequence, <a id="eq:mass-liminf"></a>$$\label{eq:mass-liminf}
                 \liminf_k\delta_k\log_L\frac1{m(b_k)}\ge T.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-mass-lower-length"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-mass-lower-length</summary>

*Proof.* If the assertion failed, a further subsequence and numbers $0<w<T$, $\eta>0$ would satisfy $$\delta_k\log_L\frac1{m(b_k)}\le w-2\eta.$$ Take $n_k=\lfloor L^{w/\delta_k}\rfloor$. Then, for all large $k$, $$n_km(b_k)\ge L^{\eta/\delta_k}
                    \ge n_k^{\eta/(2w)}.$$ The coefficients of $F_{n_k}$ and $H_{n_k}$ have uniformly bounded absolute sums. Lemma <a href="#lem:mass-loop-covariance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mass-loop-covariance">27</a> makes their covariance tend to zero, because an exponential in a positive power of $n_k$ dominates the polynomial in <a href="#eq:mass-covariance-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-covariance-decay">[eq:mass-covariance-decay]</a>. This contradicts <a href="#eq:mass-positive-pinned" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-positive-pinned">[eq:mass-positive-pinned]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Zero coefficient on an interval gives strong decay

We next use the part of the profile above $T$. The argument is a finite-scale version of the circulation proof of <a id="pm-cite-82"></a>[[X](#ref-X), Proposition 2.6]. Its relevant input is especially simple: on a compact interval of logarithmic scales with zero coefficient, the transforms of bounded affine tests on free squares tend uniformly to one. This is Corollary <a href="#cor:profile-zero-uniform" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:profile-zero-uniform">22</a>; it follows from vanishing affine variance and the uniform reference exponential moments. The tilt below is fixed before the limit in $k$.

<a id="stmt-prop-mass-polynomial"></a>
<div id="prop:mass-polynomial" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:mass-polynomial"></a>
**Proposition 29**. *Fix $w'>T$, positive integers $r_k$ satisfying $\delta_k\log_Lr_k\to w'$, and constants $0<c_0<C_0<\infty$. For every $K>0$, all sufficiently large $k$ satisfy $$\label{eq:mass-polynomial}
 \sup_{c_0r_k\le\|x\|_\infty\le C_0r_k}C_{b_k}(x)
                                      \le r_k^{-K}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-mass-polynomial"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-mass-polynomial</summary>

<a id="eq:mass-annulus-count"></a>
*Proof.* Choose $T<u<v<w'$, and use radii $$\mathcal R_k=\{4^j:j\in\mathbb Z,\quad
                         u\le\delta_k\log_L(4^j)\le v\}.$$ Their number $N_k$ obeys $$\label{eq:mass-annulus-count}
 N_k=\frac{v-u}{\delta_k\log_L4}+O(1),\qquad
 \frac{N_k}{\log r_k}\longrightarrow
                          q:=\frac{v-u}{w'\log4}>0.$$ For $R\in\mathcal R_k$, the annuli $R<|z|<2R$ about the source at $0$ are disjoint. Their maximal radius is $o(r_k)$; therefore they avoid the other source $x$, uniformly over the displacements in the proposition. Figure <a href="#fig:mass-geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:mass-geometry">2</a> compares this annular geometry with the pinned geometry used for the lower bound.

<figure id="fig:mass-geometry">

<figcaption>Two finite geometries for the mass comparison. Left: a primary pin region of positive width surrounds $D$. A loop contributing to the covariance of two separated density smears must have diameter at least their separation. The displayed loop is schematic. Right: geometrically spaced annuli surround one spin insertion and remain inside the separation scale of the other. Only two annuli are drawn. Their logarithmic scales lie in an interval where the height coefficient vanishes, so a fixed circulation tilt has arbitrarily small fluctuation cost on each annulus.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="eq:mass-annulus-form"></a>
<a id="eq:mass-deterministic-circulation"></a>
Choose a nonnegative, nonzero smooth radial function $\chi$ supported in $(1,2)$, and let $$p(z)=\chi(|z|)\nabla\arg z,
       \qquad
       D=\int_{\mathbb R^2}p(z)\cdot\nabla\arg z\,dz>0.$$ This is a smooth divergence-free form, compactly supported in the unit annulus. Choose its radial stream function $\psi$, constant on each complementary component, with $\nabla^\perp\psi=p$. At scale $R$, sample $\psi_R(z)=\psi(z/R)$ on the faces of the primary height graph and take its rotated discrete difference. Denote the resulting edge form by $\eta_R$, choosing its sign to agree with the positive source. Thus, on an edge with midpoint $z_e$, its corresponding coordinate is $R^{-1}p(z_e/R)+O(R^{-2})$. It has zero discrete divergence, $$\label{eq:mass-annulus-form}
 \max_e|\eta_R(e)|\le C/R,\qquad
 \sum_e|\eta_R(e)|^2\le C.$$ For any affine height difference $\nabla h+\alpha$ representing the unit spin twist at $0,x$, discrete summation by parts gives $$\label{eq:mass-deterministic-circulation}
 \sum_e\eta_R(e)(\nabla h+\alpha)_e=D_R,
                       \qquad D_R\longrightarrow D>0.$$ Indeed the gradient part pairs to zero. The remaining pairing is the difference of the stream-function values at the two source faces, multiplied by their circulations $\pm2\pi$. The stream function is constant near each source, so there is no dependence on the height configuration or on the path chosen for $\alpha$. The convergence in <a href="#eq:mass-deterministic-circulation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-deterministic-circulation">[eq:mass-deterministic-circulation]</a> also follows directly by Riemann sums; its error is uniform here because the second source lies outside every annulus. Fix $d_{\rm circ}>0$ so that $D_R\ge d_{\rm circ}$ for all sufficiently large radii.

<a id="eq:mass-tilted-ratio"></a>
We make the partition-ratio estimate at fixed $k,x$ in a finite free spin box large enough to contain all these annuli in its interior. Write $Z_\alpha$ and $Z_0$ for the twisted and centered height sums, both with exterior face height zero. Free-boundary duality gives $C_{G,b_k}(0,x)=Z_\alpha/Z_0$. For any fixed $\lambda>0$, insertion of the deterministic tilt from <a href="#eq:mass-deterministic-circulation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-deterministic-circulation">[eq:mass-deterministic-circulation]</a> gives the exact identity $$\label{eq:mass-tilted-ratio}
 e^{\lambda\sum_{R\in\mathcal R_k}D_R}C_{G,b_k}(0,x)
 =\frac{Z_\alpha\left(
       e^{\lambda\sum_R(\eta_R,\nabla h+\alpha)}\right)}{Z_0}.$$ Here $Z_\alpha(F)$ means the unnormalized twisted sum with insertion $F$. We will bound the right side independently of the outer spin box.

Fix a small relative cell size $t>0$. Cover the support of each $\eta_R$ by square primary cells of side $m_R=\lfloor tR\rfloor$, with disjoint vertex sets, leaving individual edges between cells as seam increments. The support of $\chi$ has positive distance from the annular boundary. Thus for sufficiently small fixed $t$, every cell meeting this support lies inside the annulus, avoids both sources, and is simply connected. There are $O(t^{-2})$ such cells at each radius. Edges not in a retained cell and carrying a nonzero tilt number $O_t(R)$.

Release the endpoint constraints between these cells and the seam increments, and release all remaining constraints outside the cells. The tilted affine-to-centered comparison <a id="pm-cite-83"></a>[[X](#ref-X), Lemma 2.2(ii)] bounds the normalized expression in <a href="#eq:mass-tilted-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-tilted-ratio">[eq:mass-tilted-ratio]</a> by the product of the cell transforms and the independent whole-edge seam transforms. Untilted factors on the rest of the graph have affine-to-centered ratio at most one. On a retained cell the connection is an integral gradient, $\alpha=\nabla\kappa$; reindexing $h'=h+\kappa$ removes it from both the weight and the statistic being tilted. Accordingly the factor for that cell is the centered free transform $$\mathbb E_{Q,b_k}^{\rm free}
                     \exp\{\lambda(\eta_R,\nabla h)_Q\}.$$ In particular, this gauge change introduces no additional real exponential factor. Released constant modes are taken modulo common translations; the edge-difference tests annihilate every such constant. Equivalently, one can retain vanishing quadratic penalties on these constants in both sectors and cancel their identical factors before removing them. No outer-box normalizer is present in the resulting bound.

<a id="eq:mass-affine-cell-reduction"></a>
We now estimate the factors for one annulus. The total squared coefficient cost of its seam increments is $O_t(R^{-1})$ by <a href="#eq:mass-annulus-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-annulus-form">[eq:mass-annulus-form]</a>. The Bessel increment transform is $\exp\{b_k(\cosh(2\pi s)-1)\}$, so their combined logarithmic cost is $O_{t,\lambda}(R^{-1})$, uniformly in $k$. On each retained square replace $\eta_R$ by its value at a chosen point of the square. Smoothness of $p$ makes the total squared cost of the coefficient error over all cells $O(t^2)+o_R(1)$. The bounded-flow estimate <a href="#eq:bounded-flow-mgf" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bounded-flow-mgf">[eq:bounded-flow-mgf]</a> and Hölder’s inequality with exponent two therefore give $$\begin{aligned}
 &\sum_Q\log\mathbb E_{Q,b_k}^{\rm free}
                  e^{\lambda(\eta_R,\nabla h)_Q}
 \notag\\[-2pt]
 &\qquad\le
 \frac12\sum_Q\log\mathbb E_{Q,b_k}^{\rm free}
                    e^{2\lambda(\bar\eta_{R,Q},\nabla h)_Q}
                       +C_\lambda t^2+o_{t,\lambda,R}(1),
 \label{eq:mass-affine-cell-reduction}
\end{aligned}$$ where $\bar\eta_{R,Q}$ is the constant coefficient on $Q$. The error coefficients multiplied by the fixed tilt are uniformly bounded, as required by <a href="#eq:bounded-flow-mgf" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bounded-flow-mgf">[eq:bounded-flow-mgf]</a>. That estimate bounds each error transform by its squared coefficient cost. Summing the costs gives the total $C_\lambda t^2$ in <a href="#eq:mass-affine-cell-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-affine-cell-reduction">[eq:mass-affine-cell-reduction]</a>.

<a id="eq:mass-annulus-cost"></a>
Each constant test in the sum is a linear combination of the two square affine observations defining $a_{m_R}(b_k)$. Its coefficient vector has size at most $C_\lambda m_R/R\le C_\lambda t$. Translations of the square do not change its law. Moreover, $$\delta_k\log_Lm_R
            =\delta_k\log_LR+\delta_k\log_Lt+o(1).$$ Thus these scales lie in a slightly enlarged compact subinterval of $(T,w')$ for all large $k$. By Corollary <a href="#cor:profile-zero-uniform" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:profile-zero-uniform">22</a>, the transforms of these affine tests tend uniformly to one, uniformly also in their bounded coefficient vectors. Since the number of cells is uniformly bounded once $t$ is fixed, their total logarithmic cost is $o_k(1)$ uniformly over $R\in\mathcal R_k$. The reference estimates and $\min\mathcal R_k\to\infty$ also make all seam and discretization errors uniform. We obtain, for every fixed $\lambda,t$, the per-annulus bound $$\label{eq:mass-annulus-cost}
           \log(\text{product of its transforms})
                             \le C_\lambda t^2+o_k(1).$$

Choose $\lambda$ first, and then $t$ so small that $C_\lambda t^2\le\lambda d_{\rm circ}/4$. For all sufficiently large $k$, the uniform error in <a href="#eq:mass-annulus-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-annulus-cost">[eq:mass-annulus-cost]</a> is at most $\lambda d_{\rm circ}/4$. Equations <a href="#eq:mass-tilted-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-tilted-ratio">[eq:mass-tilted-ratio]</a> and <a href="#eq:mass-annulus-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-annulus-count">[eq:mass-annulus-count]</a> imply $$C_{G,b_k}(0,x)
                         \le\exp\{-\lambda d_{\rm circ}N_k/2\}.$$ This estimate holds uniformly in every sufficiently large outer spin box, at the fixed values of $b_k,r_k,x$. We may therefore first take the prescribed free-box thermodynamic limit. The resulting estimate for $C_{b_k}(x)$ is uniform in the indicated range of $x$. Finally choose the fixed $\lambda$ large enough that $\lambda d_{\rm circ}q/4>K$. Equation <a href="#eq:mass-annulus-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-annulus-count">[eq:mass-annulus-count]</a> then gives <a href="#eq:mass-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-polynomial">[eq:mass-polynomial]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### A finite-size criterion and completion of the proof

<a id="eq:mass-lieb-rivasseau"></a>
The Lieb–Rivasseau inequality for plane rotators <a id="pm-cite-84"></a>[[Lieb](#ref-Lieb), [Rivasseau](#ref-Rivasseau)], in the form stated in <a id="pm-cite-85"></a>[[vEL](#ref-vEL), Lemma 17], says that for a finite subgraph $H\subset G$, $u\in H$, and $v\notin H$, $$\label{eq:mass-lieb-rivasseau}
 C_{G,b}(u,v)\le\sum_{z\in\partial H}
                       C_{H,b}(u,z)C_{G,b}(z,v).$$ Here $\partial H$ is the set of vertices of $H$ adjacent to a vertex outside $H$; the first factor is the correlation with free boundary on $H$. We give the iteration so its conclusion refers to exactly the axis mass of this paper.

<a id="stmt-lem-mass-finite-size"></a>
<div id="lem:mass-finite-size" class="lemma">

**Lemma 30**. *Let $H_r=[-r,r]^2\cap\mathbb Z^2$, and put $$S_{r,b}=\sum_{z\in\partial H_r}C_{H_r,b}(0,z).$$ If $S_{r,b}\le1/2$, then $m(b)\ge(\log2)/r$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-mass-finite-size"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-mass-finite-size</summary>

*Proof.* At fixed $b,r$, take the free-box thermodynamic limit in <a href="#eq:mass-lieb-rivasseau" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-lieb-rivasseau">[eq:mass-lieb-rivasseau]</a>. For a target $se_1$, apply the inequality successively in translates of $H_r$ centered at the boundary point reached in the previous step. Each step moves the center by at most $r$ in the supremum norm. Hence at least $\lfloor s/r\rfloor-1$ iterations are possible before the target can belong to the next translated square. Translation invariance and $C_b\le1$ give $$C_b(se_1)\le
                    S_{r,b}^{\lfloor s/r\rfloor-1}.$$ Taking $-s^{-1}\log$ and then $s\to\infty$ proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-mass-upper-length"></a>
<div id="prop:mass-upper-length" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:mass-limsup"></a>
**Proposition 31**. *Along the extracted sequence, $$\label{eq:mass-limsup}
                 \limsup_k\delta_k\log_L\frac1{m(b_k)}\le T.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-mass-upper-length"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-mass-upper-length</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $w'>T$, and set $r_k=\lfloor L^{w'/\delta_k}\rfloor$. Proposition <a href="#prop:mass-polynomial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:mass-polynomial">29</a>, with any fixed $K>1$, and finite-to-infinite monotonicity imply $$S_{r_k,b_k}\le\sum_{z\in\partial H_{r_k}}C_{b_k}(z)
                     \le8r_k\,r_k^{-K}<\frac12$$ for all sufficiently large $k$. Thus Lemma <a href="#lem:mass-finite-size" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mass-finite-size">30</a> yields $m(b_k)\ge(\log2)/r_k$. Consequently $$\limsup_k\delta_k\log_L\frac1{m(b_k)}
 \le\lim_k\delta_k\log_L\frac{r_k}{\log2}=w'.$$ Letting $w'\downarrow T$ proves <a href="#eq:mass-limsup" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-limsup">[eq:mass-limsup]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-mass-upper-length-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-mass-upper-length</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Start with any sequence $b_k<b_c$ tending to $b_c$. The construction in Sections <a href="#sec:trajectory" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:trajectory">4</a> and <a href="#sec:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:profile">5</a> admits a subsequence with the profile <a href="#eq:mass-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-profile">[eq:mass-profile]</a>. Its transition point is $$T=\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{3\mathcal P}},$$ where $\mathcal P\in(0,\infty)$ is independent of the extracted sequence. Propositions <a href="#prop:mass-lower-length" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:mass-lower-length">28</a> and <a href="#prop:mass-upper-length" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:mass-upper-length">31</a> prove <a href="#eq:mass-scale-goal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-scale-goal">[eq:mass-scale-goal]</a> on that subsequence. If the asserted limit failed for the original parameter approach, there would be a sequence on which its distance from $T$ stayed bounded below; applying the same extraction to that sequence would give a contradiction. Therefore the logarithmic-scale limit holds as $b\uparrow b_c$ without extraction. Converting from base $L$ to natural logarithms gives $$\lim_{b\uparrow b_c}\sqrt{b_c-b}\log\frac1{m(b)}
       =(\log L)\,T
       =\frac{\pi\log L}{\sqrt{3\mathcal P}}
       =:A_{\rm XY}\in(0,\infty).$$ All spin correlations used above are limits of the prescribed free spin boxes before their separation tends to infinity. Thus the mass in this identity is exactly the mass in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. ◻

</details>

    {
      "caption": "The two limits that determine the correlation length. Left: the explicit rescaled trajectory, with $k=\\sqrt{3\\mathcal P}$, has its outgoing pole at $w = T$. Right: the finite-scale free-height coefficient has a step at the same value. No value at the jump is asserted. Positive covariance below $T$ and circulation estimates above $T$ give the two bounds on $δlog_{L}ξ(b)$.",
      "figure_id": "fig:flow-profile",
      "pdf_page": 4,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:flow-profile",
          "exact_quote": "The two limits that determine the correlation length. Left: the explicit rescaled trajectory, with $k=\\sqrt{3\\mathcal P}$, has its outgoing pole at $w=T$. Right: the finite-scale free-height coefficient has a step at the same value. No valu",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,>=Latex,font=\\small]\n\\begin{scope}[xshift=0cm,yshift=1.9cm,x=1.42cm,y=.39cm]\n \\draw[->] (0,-4.2)--(0,4.7) node[above] {$X/k,\\ Y/k$};\n \\draw[->] (0,0)--(3.42,0) node[right] {$kw$};\n \\draw[densely dashed,gray] (3.14159,-4.1)--(3.14159,4.3);\n \\node[below right] at (3.14159,0) {$\\pi$};\n \\node[below] at (1.5708,0) {$\\pi/2$};\n \\draw[blue!65!black,very thick,domain=.26:2.88,samples=100]\n  plot (\\x,{cos(\\x*180/pi)/sin(\\x*180/pi)});\n \\draw[orange!75!black,very thick,domain=.26:2.88,samples=100]\n  plot (\\x,{1/sin(\\x*180/pi)});\n \\node[blue!65!black,fill=white,inner sep=1pt] at (2.2,-2.8) {$\\cot(kw)$};\n \\node[orange!75!black,fill=white,inner sep=1pt] at (1.75,2.5) {$\\csc(kw)$};\n \\draw[->,blue!65!black,thick] (1.17,.43)--(1.34,.235);\n \\node[align=center] at (1.57,-5.8) {Limiting scalar trajectory};\n\\end{scope}\n\\begin{scope}[xshift=7.2cm,yshift=.3cm,x=1.45cm,y=2.8cm]\n \\draw[->] (0,0)--(3.4,0) node[right] {$w$};\n \\draw[->] (0,0)--(0,1.35) node[above] {$\\mathcal A(w)$};\n \\draw[densely dashed,gray] (1.85,0)--(1.85,1.15);\n \\node[below] at (1.85,0) {$T=\\pi/k$};\n \\draw[blue!65!black,very thick] (.06,1)--(1.85,1);\n \\draw[blue!65!black,very thick] (1.85,.015)--(3.1,.015);\n \\draw[blue!65!black,fill=white,thick] (1.85,1) circle[x radius=.045,y radius=.023];\n \\draw[blue!65!black,fill=white,thick] (1.85,.015) circle[x radius=.045,y radius=.023];\n \\node[left] at (0,1) {$8\\pi$};\n \\node[above,align=center,font=\\footnotesize] at (.87,.09) {Positive pinned\\\\covariance};\n \\node[above,align=center,font=\\footnotesize] at (2.66,.35) {Vanishing\\\\height variance};\n \\node[align=center] at (1.55,-.23) {Limiting height coefficient};\n\\end{scope}\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two side-by-side plots.\nLeft plot: Horizontal axis labeled $kw$ with ticks at $\\pi/2$ and $\\pi$. Vertical axis labeled $X/k, Y/k$. Two curves are plotted: an orange curve labeled $\\csc(kw)$ which is convex, positive, and diverges as $kw \\to 0$ and $kw \\to \\pi$; and a blue curve labeled $\\cot(kw)$ decreasing from $+\\infty$ to $-\\infty$, crossing zero at $kw = \\pi/2$. Below the left plot is the label \"Limiting scalar trajectory\".\nRight plot: Horizontal axis labeled $w$ with a tick at $T = \\pi/k$. Vertical axis labeled $\\mathcal{A}(w)$ with a tick at $8\\pi$. A step function is shown in blue: constant at value $8\\pi$ for $w \\in [0, T)$, followed by a vertical dashed line down to $0$ at $w = T = \\pi/k$, and constant at $0$ for $w > T$. An arrow points from the text \"Positive pinned covariance\" to the segment below $T$, and another arrow points from \"Vanishing height variance\" to the segment above $T$. Below the right plot is the label \"Limiting height coefficient\"."
    },
    {
      "caption": "Two finite geometries for the mass comparison. Left: a primary pin region of positive width surrounds $D$. A loop contributing to the covariance of two separated density smears must have diameter at least their separation. The displayed loop is schematic. Right: geometrically spaced annuli surround one spin insertion and remain inside the separation scale of the other. Only two annuli are drawn. Their logarithmic scales lie in an interval where the height coefficient vanishes, so a fixed circulation tilt has arbitrarily small fluctuation cost on each annulus.",
      "figure_id": "fig:mass-geometry",
      "pdf_page": 49,
      "source_evidence": [
        {

          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Latex,font=\\small]\n\\begin{scope}[xshift=2.3cm,yshift=2.3cm]\n \\fill[gray!18] (-2,-2) rectangle (2,2);\n \\fill[white] (-1.5,-1.5) rectangle (1.5,1.5);\n \\draw[gray!70] (-2,-2) rectangle (2,2);\n \\draw[gray!70] (-1.5,-1.5) rectangle (1.5,1.5);\n \\node at (0,1.75) {$h=0$};\n \\node[anchor=north east] at (1.48,1.48) {$D$};\n \\fill[blue!18] (-1.03,-.38) rectangle (-.53,.38);\n \\fill[orange!22] (.53,-.38) rectangle (1.03,.38);\n \\node at (-.78,0) {$f$};\n \\node at (.78,0) {$g$};\n \\draw[blue!65!black,thick] (0,0) ellipse[x radius=1.27cm,y radius=.82cm];\n \\draw[->,blue!65!black,thick] (-.36,.786)--(-.1,.818);\n \\draw[<->] (-.53,-1.13)--(.53,-1.13) node[midway,below] {$c n$};\n \\node[align=center] at (0,-2.5) {Pinned covariance below $T$};\n\\end{scope}\n\\begin{scope}[xshift=8.6cm,yshift=2.3cm]\n \\fill[blue!12,even odd rule] (0,0) circle (1.68) (0,0) circle (1.4);\n \\fill[blue!12,even odd rule] (0,0) circle (.42) (0,0) circle (.35);\n \\draw[blue!55!black] (0,0) circle (1.4);\n \\draw[blue!55!black] (0,0) circle (1.68);\n \\draw[blue!55!black] (0,0) circle (.35);\n \\draw[blue!55!black] (0,0) circle (.42);\n \\fill (0,0) circle (.035) node[below left] {$0$};\n \\fill (2.6,0) circle (.035) node[right] {$x$};\n \\draw[dashed,gray] (.45,0)--(2.53,0);\n \\node at (.9,1.05) {$4R$};\n \\node[below right,font=\\footnotesize] at (.17,-.42) {$R$};\n \\draw[->,blue!60!black] (-.33,.86) arc[start angle=111,end angle=145,radius=.92];\n \\node[align=center] at (0,-2.08) {$\\delta\\log_LR_i\\in[u,v]\\subset(T,w')$};\n \\node[align=center] at (0,-2.5) {Circulation tilts above $T$};\n\\end{scope}\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two schematic diagrams side-by-side.\nLeft diagram: A rectangular domain with top boundary labeled $h = 0$. Inside is a rounded region labeled $D$ containing two small circular disks labeled $f$ and $g$. A blue oriented loop with an arrow encloses both $f$ and $g$ inside $D$. A double-headed horizontal arrow below the bottom border of the region indicates width $cn$. Below this diagram is the label \"Pinned covariance below $T$\".\nRight diagram: An annular configuration centered at a point labeled $0$. Dashed radial arrows indicate inner radius $R$ and outer radius $4R$. Two concentric rings (blue and purple) are drawn around $0$. A dashed horizontal line extends to the right to a second point labeled $x$. Below the diagram is the expression $\\delta \\log_L R_i \\in [u, v] \\subset (T, w')$ and the label \"Circulation tilts above $T$\"."
    }
